ParlaMint-DK_20201217104804	Thank you. Then we come to the answer. Here you go, Prime Minister.
ParlaMint-DK_20201217104814	Thank you for that and thank the rapporteurs for having called a debate again on a reception centre. This is an important issue and, as has just been said, the challenges are to be seen. Even in a year of Corona, which has put a great deal of pressure on Europe's borders, there is still a lot of pressure. This is a problem for Europe, and it is also a problem for those who risk their lives on the road. This year alone, some 900 people are estimated to have drowned or disappeared in the Mediterranean. It's deeply tragic. And even for those who survive, the tough journey is often quite hopeless, for there are many migrants who do not have a legal errand in Europe, and there are many who, in addition, are denied their asylum applications. We have a European asylum system that has actually collapsed. That is why we need a new one, in which, in short, it is we Europeans who decide the development, and not the traffickers of human beings, that is to say, a new asylum system, which must be more humane and fairer. We must help people where they are, lift our international responsibilities, but keep immigration under control for Europe. In the government, we maintain that asylum treatment outside Europe must be a key element in a new solution. We think this is the best way to break the incentives. In September, the government appointed a migration ambassador for the first time. We have allocated more resources to work. We are in close dialogue with the like-minded about the problems of the current system, which has collapsed, and also about the need for new solutions, and we ourselves, in the midst of the corona, have continued our dialogue with relevant countries. There is now agreement in more and more countries, both within the EU and outside the EU, that the current system is not working and that problems need to be resolved more closely in the neighbourhoods. In other words, more and more people share our analysis. Governments inside and outside Europe are now talking about visions that are similar to the discussion we have in Denmark, and in my own talks with colleagues I sense that there is increasing support for thinking new in this area too. In September, the Commission presented a proposal for a new asylum and migration pact for Europe. We think there are proposals in the pact that are moving in the right direction. There is a greater focus on repatriations now, there is a greater focus on cooperation with countries of origin and transit, and there is also, fortunately, a stronger focus on strengthening external borders. However, in our opinion, the Commission is not proceeding drastically enough when it comes to solving the basic problems. The new pact, for example, contains: not proposals to move asylum treatment to third countries. We did not expect that to happen either, but it is of course a pity that the Commission's proposal is not more innovative. We continue to press for longer-term solutions, and we will continue and vigorously pursue the objective of transferring asylum seekers to asylum treatment outside Europe. We will do that with others who see things the same way we do. There are many who say that this is not realistic and that it cannot be done. And I recognize, as I have done in other debates, that it is difficult. On the other hand, I do not recognise that this is an impossible task, and the alternative is to allow a dysfunctional asylum system to continue. The assessment is that it is possible to do this within our international commitments. We are therefore in the process of laying the foundations for a new system. This foundation is presented in a bill we are presenting in the new year. It must ensure that we can, I know, that many in this Parliament share our basic analysis that the asylum system has collapsed, that it does not work. I also know that there is broad support for us to move forward with ideas that are at least similar to those of the government. That is why I would like to call in the usual way for cooperation in this area. I also recognise impatience, and I really think it was very fair on the part of the rapporteur to say that previous governments have also tried to do this. Thank you for the floor.

ParlaMint-DK_20201217110253	Thank you. And let me start by saying that I fully agree with the Prime Minister that the refugee policy debate remains important, even though the current asylum figures are record low. Right now we're at about 60%. of the number of asylums for 2019, which was, moreover, the lowest number of asylums since 2008. Every day last year, on average, about three people drowned or disappeared across the Mediterranean in their attempt to reach Europe • three people every day. While people are drowning in the Mediterranean or being subjected to attacks on the roads up the Sahara against the Mediterranean, for many years there has been a political discussion in Europe about how to distribute the people who survive the trip through the desert and with the dinghy over the Mediterranean and put their feet on European soil and say: Asylum. But we have said this before and it is and will be a patchy solution to distribute asylum seekers in this way, and I do not understand why we do not spend more time finding more sustainable solutions. If we look around, unfortunately today there are major problems with a growing illegal subclass around Europe. It is people who have come here who have been refused asylum or who have been given a visa that has now expired but who refuse to go home. Instead, they go underground, live under the bridges or in railway stations and work illegally without rights... it can be in the cleaning industry, in restaurants, as bottle collectors or by feeding themselves by theft or by selling drugs. We have probably all heard of illegal migrant camps in Paris or other European cities, and that is not how it should end up in Denmark. But rejected asylum seekers and people who have come here without a visa and who do not go home, even though it has expired, are the product of the current immigration policy. And there is therefore a need to get to grips with the root of the nettle. There is a need to remove completely the reason why people are initially willing to jump on a dinghy and embark on a life-threatening journey. Because there is not and should not be free immigration into Europe. We need better control over who comes in. Access to Europe must be controlled by the states themselves, not by traffickers. This is precisely the way in which we are going to debate today, in other words a new asylum system. So thank you for calling today's debate. We have said from the beginning, since we presented our programme back in February 2018, that it is a difficult task. However, we will insist that we cannot look passively at the current situation simply continuing or escalating. Although the asylum figures in Denmark are quiet at the moment, we can risk ending up again in a situation where asylum figures are rising, and then we cannot afford to have just been passive and not to have acted on this. There is a need to rethink our entire asylum system so that fewer migrants are taken hostage in the business model of traffickers; so that fewer people die and are subjected to attacks along the routes; but also so that Denmark and other European societies are not threatened with our internal security, for example. of rejected asylum seekers, or threatened with our cohesion, by the fact that immigration is too large for our country. We continue to believe in the government that the best way to do this is to put an end to the current asylum system, which encourages migrants to take the dangerous journey across the Mediterranean Sea, and by moving asylum procedures and the protection of refugees outside the European Union. We believe that we can help more and better if we do it closer to the conflicts. That is also why we have decided to increase support for the neighbourhood. And we are also going to go further in this direction when it comes to prioritising development funds. In addition, the government wants primary access to the protection of refugees in Denmark to be through legal roads, as is the case with controlled resettlement through the UN, or as we do, for example. know it from our business and study schemes. Quota refugees from neighbouring areas are fairer and more appropriate than the redistribution of spontaneous asylum seekers and irregular migrants who have been helped on their way to this end by traffickers. We therefore want to pursue a policy in which we are more in control of ourselves, how many and what foreigners come to Denmark, while at the same time wishing to take international responsibility. We have given ourselves a very, very difficult task in moving asylum procedures away from the European Union. To say the least, setting up a reception centre in a third country is not something you can fix with a fingertips. However, we believe that this is the right solution to really make up for the loss of control caused by illegal migration today. And I believe that we must be prepared to go very far to make our proposals a reality. That is why, as the first Danish government, the government is doing the legal legwork which confirms that it is possible to do this within our international obligations, that is to say to move asylum procedures. This means that we can put forward a legislative proposal at the beginning of the new year, giving us the necessary national legal basis to use to transfer asylum seekers to a country outside the European Union. The legislative proposal lays the legal basis for our vision of a new and fairer asylum system, and it is one step on a long road, but it is one step further than we have been before. In addition to creating the national legal basis, as the Prime Minister has just mentioned, we have set up a task force. It's a group of officials who are working dedicatedly on putting full steam on the boilers and getting these visions realized. We have appointed a new ambassador to strengthen dialogue with partner countries both inside and outside Europe, which will help to break the business model of traffickers. And through development aid, we have started to support development projects along migration routes, among others. focusing on making countries better able to protect their own borders against illegal migration and on supporting countries in repatriating migrants to their countries of origin, but also with the aim of getting into dialogue with governments on common interests in gaining more control over migratory flows and perhaps laying the foundations for further cooperation. The challenges must not be dealt with only when they are knocking on the door at the Danish border. We need to work more strategically in the neighbourhood and along migration routes, and very much in cooperation with other European countries, and that is exactly what we have done. For example: I would like to mention that we have spent 15 million. kr. In the Western Balkans, migrants who are still only on the move towards the European Union will be able to return home voluntarily and with a little economic help, for example. to Pakistan, and be reintegrated into society there, with a bag of money from Europe in the luggage. In addition, we support, among other things: also an information campaign in the Horn of Africa, targeted at asylum seekers and other people considering migrating irregularly to Europe, with a further 16 million. kr.; we have just launched this. The campaign must contribute to providing credible information about the journey itself, but also about conditions if they are to arrive. More than half of the Eritreans who have seen a similar campaign were less likely to travel with the traffickers after receiving such honest reports from those who had already taken the trip. Finally, we have supported similar projects in Tunisia and Rwanda to deal with migration. In Tunisia, it was about helping to keep their borders, among others. Algeria, and in Rwanda, it is about the resettlement of some of the vulnerable asylum seekers and refugees who have ended up in some of these truly terrible detention centres in Libya and giving them a future in another African country. I really think this is commendable by the Rwandan Government, and I think that as a European country we should support it. The projects must help to reduce migration flows before they reach Europe. And that is why it is also a step in the right direction in the Danish Government's efforts for a fair asylum system. Several of these projects have also been launched in cooperation with other European countries. As expected, the European Commission's proposal for a new Common Asylum and Migration Pact did not include a proposal for reception centres in third countries. And even though the Commission's proposals have other good tactics that run counter to our policy on several points, among other things. improving the repatriation of rejected asylum seekers and creating more efficient external borders and strengthening efforts on migration routes, the measures are not enough if we are to succeed in breaking the unhealthy structure of the current asylum system. This is precisely why we in Denmark will continue our efforts to explore the possibility of creating a new asylum system with partner countries both in Europe and along migration routes. And I would like to say very frankly that, of course, I do not know the date when our ambition to move asylum procedures to a country outside the European Union can be met. However, we insist that we want to change the asylum system that benefits neither the countries of Europe nor the world's refugees, and we are pursuing our policy and working seriously with it. I am also convinced that most parties today think that the best thing would be if we could get things moving quickly. Of course, we think so ourselves, but that is not the case with difficult tasks like this one. It's a big and a complicated task. We have said that since we proposed it, but we are working hard. It is true that the corona situation has slowed down a great deal, among other things. Because we don't have the same opportunities to travel. I had to, for example. even had been in Africa a few weeks ago, and it did not succeed. But it has by no means cancelled our ambitions; we are working everything we can within the framework of the current situation as regards travel activity. With those words, I would like to thank you for the opportunity to discuss this matter. We look forward, of course, to the debate and look forward to hearing the contributions of the other parties in the Folketing as well.

ParlaMint-DK_20201217112956	Thank you, President. In the Social Democratic Party, we would like to prevent us from experiencing new migratory and refugee flows such as those we experienced shortly after the bourgeois government came to power in 2015. As you are all aware, we are therefore working to find a model for a reception centre outside Europe. The current asylum system is not working; it is dysfunctional and unfair, and it is an asylum system that takes into account money and physically strong asylum seekers and refugees. The weakest refugees are left without help in the neighbourhoods. That is why we want to reform the current asylum system so that we have a fairer and more sustainable asylum system. The asylum treatment must, as far as possible, take place outside Denmark's borders. Our plan to set up reception centres in third countries is that spontaneous asylum seekers coming to Denmark should be transferred to a reception centre in a third country, and asylum procedures should then take place here. If asylum seekers are refused, they must be sent home. If they are refugees, they must be protected in the country concerned and not in Denmark. Our objective to establish reception centres in third countries will slow down the flow to Denmark, and this will ensure that access to Denmark is controlled by Denmark and not by traffickers. The current asylum system is unfair and dysfunctional; we spend far too much money to help people who are not refugees at all. We need an asylum system that is humane, realistic and fair and that removes the incentive that causes people in the Third World to take the dangerous journey towards Europe. Parliament notes that the government is working to establish a system for the transfer of asylum seekers to asylum treatment in third countries, which is ending the incentive structure that requires migrants to embark on the dangerous journey across the Mediterranean. The Danish Parliament supports national and international efforts to achieve this. At the same time, Parliament notes that it is not a prerequisite for achieving this that there is support in the EU. Parliament notes that a task force has been set up to achieve this ambition. A draft law is put forward to create the necessary legal framework for this. The camps must be run by local governments, but must be financed fully by the EU Member States, which, in return, will be assured that the countries concerned will assume responsibility for bringing together all refugees and dealing with their cases, also in accordance with the UN Human Rights and Refugees Conventions. With such a system, asylum seekers who are denied their application will be able to return home more easily and more quickly, while those who acquire refugee status will, as a starting point, receive help and protection locally. In this way, we will solve two fundamental issues: firstly, we will ensure that economic migrants who today benefit from poor cooperation in Europe are prevented from using the system, in the certainty that the road to Europe goes through another African country with similar living standards, and secondly, we will ensure that those who really need help and protection can get it both faster and closer to their homeland than today. ParlaMint-DK_20201217121255	Thank you. People in the world cross borders for many reasons. Some do it out of love, others chasing a new job, others chasing greater social security, and then there are a lot of people fleeing war, oppression and threatening climate disasters. In the Enhedslisten, we support the UN Refugee Convention and its fundamental principle that a refugee with a need for protection is entitled to protection. As one of the most prosperous countries in the world, we have an obligation in Denmark to contribute to solving the world's refugee problems. There are now more than 80 million people on the run worldwide. So instead of talking about how we can set up a reception centre for asylum seekers outside Europe, we should instead talk about whether we are at all fulfilling our international obligations and, above all, our human commitments. As a society, we have spent the last 20 years tightening up immigration policy so much that we are in a great many ways at odds with what is legal and what is reasonable at all. We see the Convention on Human Rights as a fine declaration of intent, which just does not apply equally to Denmark, as it would be too much a burden for us to take it seriously. But now it is the case that we have committed ourselves to the Convention on Human Rights, and in the Enhedslisten we do not see the Convention on Human Rights as something we should go to the edge of, but we see it as something we are standing on our shoulders. It is, of course, a minimum of rights, not a maximum of rights. If we do not respect human rights in Denmark, how should we expect all other countries to do so? After all, we see a great many examples from southern Europe, where refugees with a need for protection instead of protection experience violations and human rights violations, which we cannot in any way vouch for. When you come to Europe because you have fled from war, you should not be subjected to inhumane conditions. You should not be beaten, you should not be robbed of your belongings, you should not be subjected to torture in asylum centres or in camps, as we see in Greece right now, and you should not be deprived of your human dignity. When you come to Europe to seek asylum, you must also have the opportunity to have your asylum application examined, and then of course you must have protection if you have a need for protection. With the proposal to set up reception centres for refugees coming to Denmark in a country outside the European Union, we are doing exactly the opposite of what we have a duty to do. We in Denmark apparently find it very difficult to deal with asylum seekers without the establishment of torture-like conditions at the centres, as the Council of Europe's Torture Committee concluded at the beginning of the year. That is not just what we mean in the Enhedslisten; it is therefore also something that the Council of Europe's Torture Committee has concluded. How on earth would we then be able to ensure that the refugees whom we have a duty to protect are not exposed to even worse conditions in other countries outside the European Union? So this appears to us in the Enhedslisten as a hopeless symbol policy. We do not believe that we can park the bill or the refugee in a third country, and that is why we can only support the proposal for adoption, which Mr. Kristian Hegaard read.

ParlaMint-DK_20201217130638	Thank you. The purpose of the amendment to the law was in particular to provide for the possibility of administratively depriving foreign fighters who are outside the country of their Danish nationality if they do not thereby become stateless. Currently, in ten cases, we have deprived foreign fighters of their Danish nationality on the basis of the rules, and none of those who have been deprived of citizenship have subsequently entered Denmark so far. I am very pleased that the new rules are being applied and, as you know, the government has proposed that the sunset clause for the rules on administrative withdrawal be lifted so that we can also make future use of the rules. We are bound by some international conventions, including the stateless Convention and the National Convention, which means that we cannot deny statelessness and that disqualification can only take place in certain specified cases. This applies, among other things, when a person has acted in a manner which seriously harms the vital interests of the State. But the opportunities for disqualification and within that framework, of course, we will take advantage of to take care of Denmark. I am therefore open to going a long way if possible within our international obligations. That is precisely why, in the new year, we intend to put forward a legislative proposal to the effect that persons convicted of certain types of serious gang crime which seriously harm the vital interests of Denmark may, in the context of the judgment of the criminal court, be deprived of their Danish right of birth unless they thereby become stateless. If it can also be established in this context that there is room for extending the possibility of denying drug criminals their Danish citizenship, we would like to be ready to take advantage of that opportunity on the part of the government. However, I can say that in any case it is not enough to deny citizenship that a person has committed serious crime. It comes from our obligations. Crime can therefore only lead to disqualification if crime has such a character and gravity that it can be said to be seriously detrimental to the vital interests of the State. Since we have not yet examined the question of the disqualification of the nationality of drug criminals, I cannot immediately say whether it will be possible to put forward a legislative proposal on this subject. We cannot therefore support the motion for a resolution which states that legislation on this subject must be implemented during the current parliamentary year. ParlaMint-DK_20201217133957	Thank you for the floor. The Radical Left does not support the motion for a resolution on the withdrawal of citizenship from serious drug crime. There are two points to be made. Firstly, some Convention considerations and then considerations on possible risk of damages before the Court of Human Rights. The Convention takes account of the question of whether drug crime can be a threat to the security of the state, in so far as it is in that case that citizenship can be deprived. General crime does not upset the security of the state, and we understand that the Ministry of Foreign Affairs and Integration and the Minister have expressed their intention to make a similar proposal to amend the law on citizenship so that persons convicted of serious gang crimes may be deprived of their Danish right of birth by judgment unless they become stateless. And that will then be, we must understand, in cases where serious gang crimes are considered to be seriously detrimental to Denmark's vital interests. However, since serious crime is not to be regarded as treason from the convention, the area of this motion for a resolution will, to that extent, be infinitely small. In other words, the proposal is a little like saying that if hooliganism is terrorism, then citizenship can be deprived. But if the Left or the government makes such a rule of law, then it does not mean that hooliganism is terrorism. With regard to the recitals on the Court of Human Rights, it is in this context that it makes really poor sense for the Radical Left to implement legislation which would result in compensation being paid to drug criminals. To sum up, the Radical Left is opposed to the motion for a resolution and reminds us that until now it has been felt politically that it is not possible to do so and that, in terms of the convention, it has been thought that serious crime is not a threat to the security of the state either. It is therefore not legally possible to equate ordinary crime with terrorism, since this is what the Convention on Civil Liberties and Internal Affairs exhaustively mentions, in relation to the situations in which a State's legislation may provide for the forfeiture of citizenship, that the revocation can only be considered in the event of a course of action which seriously prejudices the vital interests of a State. So we do not support the proposal.

That is the way it should be in a constitutional state, that is to say, that it is the way it is, for all citizens. That is also what the Constitution says: that there is equality before the law, regardless of sexual orientation. No, that is not what it says in the constitution, but at least we have a principle that we should be treated equally, regardless of political beliefs, sexual orientation, social status and religious affiliation, and that, of course, should be the case, that is to say, that all people are equal before the law, and that is why it should also be the case that if you receive a penalty, you are sentenced to it in the courts, and once it has been endured, you are a citizen on an equal footing with everyone else again. Then we may have initiatives to resocialize and to decriminalize, but basically it should be the case that if you have a conviction and you have served it, you have equal rights again. If you commit seriously enough drug crime, it is already the case that you are being punished seriously. This is one of the most severe penalties we punish in Denmark. That is why the Enhedslisten cannot support this resolution, because we want to enforce the principle that everyone is equal before the law.

ParlaMint-DK_20201217141205	The debate is open. And welcome to the Minister of Foreign Affairs and Integration.
ParlaMint-DK_20201217141215	New citizens are proposing in the resolution here, among other things, that criminal foreigners should be deported consistently after the first sentence. New citizens write that the motion for a resolution does not require Denmark to withdraw from the European Convention on Human Rights, but that the proposal presupposes that by law it is decided that Denmark's international obligations, including the Convention on Human Rights, should be waived in cases of expulsion of criminal aliens. I would like to begin by saying that we agree with the proposers that criminal foreigners should be expelled as far as possible. Of course, if you abuse your stay in Denmark to commit serious crime, you have nothing to do with continuing in Danish society. However, I would also like to stress that it is also important for us to live up to our international obligations. Denmark has an interest in a strong international legal order and we must actively engage in international cooperation. And within that framework, we are working hard to ensure that the authorities have the best conditions for expelling and sending out criminal foreigners. However, a proposal to introduce an unconditional rule on expulsion after the first sentence is not something that can be implemented within the framework of our international obligations. I have also recently answered that in a committee question. For this reason alone, therefore, the government cannot support the motion for a resolution. Moreover, expulsion is a priority for both the current and the then government, which is why the Social Democracy also supported the tightening of the expulsion rules, which were adopted by a broad majority in the Folketing in May 2018. Here, among other things, there were: In the light of the case law of the Court of Human Rights, guidelines were laid down as to when the Danish courts could in general expel criminal foreigners. We have thus ensured that the courts make the greatest possible use of the expulsion rules within the framework of their obligations. And as the rules are today, it is quite fundamental that if a criminal alien can be expelled from Denmark under the expulsion rules, then there must be expulsions unless it is certain that it will be contrary to Denmark's international obligations as it stands. It is a tool that I believe creates an incentive for individuals not to commit crime, and it is also a tool, it is my very clear view that, according to the recent case law of the courts, the tightening of the expulsion rules has had the desired effect. You look, for example. in the practice of the Supreme Court over recent years, there are clear indications that the trend is that more foreigners who have committed very serious and repeated crime are being expelled despite being born in Denmark or have come here as quite young people and therefore have a strong connection with the country. It is, of course, difficult to say exactly whether the trend is due to the tightening of the expulsion rules in recent years. As you know, criminal proceedings may be a long time in the process, and the courts make a concrete and individual assessment of the circumstances of each case, but I am glad that several people are actually expelled even if they have a strong connection with Denmark. And, as I have said, the rules in force are such that when the conditions for expulsion are in place, the courts will have to refrain from expelling only if the expulsion is certain to be contrary to the conventions. I would also like to point out another issue which I think is equally important not only for the government but also for society as a whole, and this is also an issue to which the proposers are referring, namely the enforcement of expulsions. Because we need to focus on enforcement, so that the aliens who are expelled are actually also sent out of the country. So it is not enough for a judge to knock on the table; we also need to get the person on the plane. In my opinion, the political debate should perhaps also be more interested in this, i.e. how we ensure that the expulsion is effectively and swiftly enforced. And, as I have already stated in a number of contexts, it is a high priority for the government to do so and to do so as soon as possible. Foreigners without legal residence, and this of course also applies to exiles, should not stay here. And when they, and others without legal residence, choose to oppose the decision of the authorities, money is being spent that we could have spent on core welfare or in the neighbourhood, and that is a problem that we will not ignore. Decisions by the Danish authorities must, of course, be complied with; that is a fundamental principle of a Community based on the rule of law. That is why, a few months ago, in August, we established a new administration, the Home Travel Board, which must be closer to the expelled criminals and rejected asylum seekers and get more people to go home. With the establishment of the Board, we are trying to streamline and strengthen the organisation of the work in order to focus more specifically on the field of deployment, and in this connection we have taken some work from Danish Refugee Aid. In addition, during the autumn, the government has presented a number of initiatives aimed at ensuring a more effective return journey for all persons without legal residence in Denmark, including the convicted criminals. And I have recently presented a proposal for a new travel law, which includes: ensure closer follow-up and faster consequences for those who are going home. One of the initiatives of that law will actually mean that criminals expelled foreigners who serve shorter sentences of up to 4 months may be released before the end of service if an opportunity arises to send the alien immediately thereafter. This will ensure that the authorities do not miss an opportunity to send a deported foreigner. In addition, we set aside resources to give the authorities enhanced opportunities to obtain relevant data from mobile phones to better identify people and thus send them home. The initiatives are based on the existing return effort and give the new management a little more muscle in the work of getting people on the plane. By the way, with the initiatives, we are picking up a number of the proposals that we presented in our foreign initiative from the Social Democratic Party back in 2018. I would like to stress that we are very concerned that convicted criminals should not be in Denmark and that they are actually returning home. We have therefore presented a number of initiatives which we look forward to discussing with the Danish Parliament on the other side of New Year. Thank you.

So, the first framework condition is that all power parties in this country believe that a small state has more advantages than the disadvantages of international treaties, but what is the challenge of the human rights convention is that the convention itself is extremely short and imprecise. It has not been pricked out at all in the way that a Danish law has been pricked out. The reason why this has not been removed is, of course, that what Article 8 says actually means that it is laid down by judgments handed down by the European Court of Human Rights. This means that Denmark has originally signed a document which is almost empty of content, which is incredibly little in it, and then the content changes all the time, as these orders are made, and it is really elastic in meter size. So the problem is not so much that we have signed the Convention on Human Rights as it is that we have signed an unclear document, the importance of which is constantly being changed by those who have not been elected and are not responsible for any citizens. For example: the Court of Human Rights has decided that it is a human right to be free from café noise. The Court of Human Rights has also ruled that with the human rights convention in hand a citizen can overrule a local plan banning satellite dishes because one has the right to free information, so a local municipality cannot create a local plan that takes these into account. And it is quite strange, frankly, that we have a human rights convention that goes into so much detail. The second problem is that the Convention on Human Rights is part of Danish law. So that means that the state of law in Denmark is constantly being changed by some people who are not in Denmark at all and who are not elected, but because it is in Danish law, we must abide by it anyway. So solving the problems from scratch is really creating political pressure against dynamic interpretations and possibly even against the incorporation law. I don't know. I believe that there is a long way to go for us to get out of the incorporation law, but at least we can start by creating some kind of political pressure or by drawing attention to its problems. For example, if we take theft, we distinguish between use theft and simple theft. So, you come home from the city one night, you take a bike, you bike on it and put it back, it's called utility theft, while simple theft is if, as in recent history, you are one who goes out and steals cargo bikes and other expensive bikes to sell them on. I think it's a line that could be a line you drew. The same is true of the Marsbars. Is it that a young guy takes a Marsbar in a supermarket, enough to expel? I wouldn't say that at first, while a young guy who goes into the warehouse and steals the boxes with Marsbars is a hero, quite different thing. So I think it is somewhere in between, and I can reassure the rapporteur that, when it comes to serious crime and also to milder crime, we are on the side of the victims with DF.

ParlaMint-DK_20201217154248	Thank you to the authors of the SF, and I would also like to say thank you for your tenacity. Nothing has since changed our position on the question of whether it would be sensible for Denmark and for children to welcome children from the Moria camp. The answer is still the same: we do not want to participate in a redistribution from the Moria camp. I, on the other hand, share the opinion of the proposers that the situation on Lesbos is serious, but that does not alter the government's basic view that we should not create incentives for more people to move towards Europe. And that is exactly what we fear will happen, in other words, that an incentive will be created for more people to get into the boats if we start redistributing asylum seekers, including from Greece. We will not help to draw a picture of the fact that, if you risk your life and reach the EU's external border, you are guaranteed a ticket to have your asylum case dealt with in Denmark or another welfare state, for example. I do not think this is a viable solution. Instead, we must put an end to the industry of traffickers who are moving migrants to Europe and who are doing so in a way in which people are also risking their lives. We've said that many times before, and we're happy to do it again. We need to put an end to the asylum system that supports this traffic. We need to address the causes of migration a little more fundamentally, and we are in the process of doing so. I believe that this is more the way to work along migration routes, to secure external borders and to avoid redistributing. Persons who come to Denmark and apply for asylum must be sent to a third country where asylum procedures are to take place and if they need protection, they must do so locally in the third country and not in Denmark. As I have also said earlier today, the government has done the legwork that confirms that it is possible within our international obligations to move asylum procedures outside the European Union, and this has also been confirmed today by the Institute for Human Rights, which has reached the same legal conclusion. We need such a system precisely in order to make up for the loss of control caused by migration today. The government therefore wants to pursue an alien policy in which we can manage more ourselves, how many and who will come to Denmark, while at the same time wishing to take international responsibility. We believe that we can help more people closer to the conflicts by sending the funds there than by spending them on asylum and subsequent stays in Denmark. As part of that policy, we also support controlled resettlement through the UN. Resettlement of quota refugees from neighbouring areas is fairer and more appropriate than redistribution of spontaneous asylum seekers and irregular migrants. I would like to conclude by stating once again that, like other European countries, we take the situation very seriously. The Greeks are faced with a difficult task, which is made clear after the fire in the Moria camp, and that is also why we have chosen to help the Greek authorities financially and with staff and equipment. However, we still do not believe that the redistribution of asylum seekers is a viable solution. We continue to follow developments in Greece closely, just as we generally follow the challenges of tackling illegal migration closely. One final point I would like to make, given that I have the floor anyway, is that the latest information from the Greek authorities is that there are currently about 2 000 people. 140 unaccompanied minors staying at Lesbos. How much of those arrived after the fire we cannot say immediately, but immediately they should all have arrived afterwards. The remainder of the unaccompanied minors who remained in Lesbos during the Moria camp fire have been transferred to the mainland. Here they are accommodated in different ways, among others. in shelters of the type to which we have contributed from the Danish side.

Therefore, the best solution is not to bring the children to Denmark, on the contrary: we must help the children and their families in the local area in which they are already located. We therefore believe that the government must continue to make strong efforts towards those refugees and migrants who need protection in the outermost regions. This means that better facilities must be created for all the innocent children who have ended up in this terrible situation. Together with the April grant, Denmark has contributed 45 million. kr. to Greece. Together, the two funds go to secure settlements for some 4,000 unaccompanied refugee and migrant children. In the Left, therefore, we believe that with targeted help and financial support in the local areas, as many unaccompanied children as possible provide the necessary temporary help and a roof over their heads. In the light of this, the Left will vote against the resolution. Thank you for the floor.

ParlaMint-DK_20201217181230	In the motion for a resolution, the Liberal and Democratic Reformist Group proposes to include social control in the criminal code in the same way as hate crimes, so that it becomes an aggravating factor in the punishment when social control has been exercised as part of a criminal offence. The reason for the proposal is that it must be clearly stated that as a society we will not accept that there is social control in Denmark, for example. women are not allowed to go to the job centre alone or to take a job where there are male colleagues. I would like to start by stressing that the government fully agrees with the intentions behind the resolution, because there should be no doubt that negative social control is totally unacceptable. This is something that we, as a society, strongly reject; it is something that we must actively fight against, and I think we all agree on that. Having said that, the government cannot support the motion for a resolution tabled by the Left, and I would now like to explain why, even if it is a little technical. Negative social control is already punishable today. It can be punished as psychological violence, and that is why it would already have to result in a higher penalty if another crime, for example, were to occur today. physical violence or coercion is committed as part of negative social control. The fact is that it follows from the criminal code that the penalty increases if you have committed several offences, which must be judged at the same time by the courts. This means that if you are convicted of both physical and psychological violence, for example. social control, then a common penalty will be laid down in practice, which would be more stringent as a starting point than if only physical violence were to be condemned. I don't want to go into a more technical explanation of the law in these two models. This can be clarified in writing if you wish, but the fact is that the rules as they are today already take account of the fact that there must be a higher penalty if another crime is committed as part of the exercise of psychic violence in the form of negative social control. But that does not change the fact that, as I said, I entirely share the intentions of the Left here, because it is certainly also a subject of concern to the government and which I also know concerns broad parts of the Folketing. In the autumn, therefore, we launched a number of initiatives by the government to combat social control. Some of the initiatives require legislative changes and have therefore been translated into a legislative proposal which was tabled yesterday here in the Folketing. The bill contains amendments to both the penal code, as proposed here in the motion for a resolution, the Aliens Act and the Passport Act. As one of the initiatives, we propose, among other things, that negative social control should be written directly into the criminal code's provisions on psychological violence. We are doing this in order to make it clear that it can already be a criminal offence to exercise negative social control, so that it becomes clear, even in the courts. In addition, the government proposes that religious marriages of minors should be banned and that the punishment for maintaining someone in a marriage with coercion should be tightened up. It must also apply to religious marriages which are not of civil validity because we know that religious marriages in certain environments are at least as valuable as civil marriages. We all know the boring stories in the press, which include: reported on a religious divorce contract in which a Funen woman was required to sign that she would lose her right to her children, among others. if she remarried or moved more than 130 km away. We were probably more people who just had to read that article more than once to understand how absurd it really was and saw here in Denmark. I think that story in particular impressed many, and the authors also refer to it in their comments on the motion for a resolution, and I understand that. With the bill that the government has just tabled yesterday, which is due to be dealt with first on Monday, we are going to strengthen the fight against social control and, of course, I am looking forward to the debate on that bill. So to conclude: I can fully support the intentions behind the motion for a resolution here, but we cannot support the motion for a resolution. And this is primarily because negative social control is already punishable as psychological violence today, and I think that is an important signal to send out to both victims and potential perpetrators, that is to say that this behaviour is punishable in line with, for example. physical violence. We have a good principle in the penal code that we do not punish for the same two times. I am glad, however, that there is broad agreement that this is an agenda for action, and so I would also like to thank the Left very much for having drafted it here and also for joining as a party in this discussion and showing that the fight against social control and the concern in this area is far from only something that concerns and interests the left side of the Assembly. So thank you for that.

And it is not just because I am a citizen of Odense, where we have seen some of the glaring examples of social control over time. The worst have ended up in honour killings, and in the autumn shocking, but not surprising, revelations about religious imams and parallel courts completely disregarding all Danish legislation, what we have also called Shariaskillsmisser: A woman from Odense could not be allowed to divorce her husband because he simply did not accept it. And in spite of the woman's desire for divorce, the husband kept visiting her in her home and demanding sexual services... what I call rape. Because the woman wasn't allowed to say no, and according to the imam, they were still married. We also have examples where women cannot divorce because they have to pay a dowry of hundreds of thousands of kroner or where they are not allowed to see their children if they move, for example. Just a mile away from their husband. The intention is to keep the woman in this iron grip so she doesn't shame her family. As I have said before, it is quite incredible that despite corona and travel restrictions, there has never been more access to Middle Eastern and Medieval conditions so close. Social control, forced marriages and marriages of minors have been going on for far too long, not only in Odense, but also elsewhere in Denmark: in immigrant environments where the biggest attacks on equality in Denmark happen over and over again, where women are not allowed to go alone at the job centre or go to work where there are male colleagues. A study has shown that young women with a non-Western background experience significantly less self-determination and freedom compared to other young people. We will not accept this, because in Denmark we have equality and we have equal opportunities. In addition, the study has also shown that young people who are exposed to this kind of social control are at greater risk of mental maltreatment, which is not so strange. That is why we are obliged to protect the women and children who are experiencing this. It is they who are the greatest victims and we must support their fight for freedom. Their invaders are Muslim darkmen who understand only one language, and that is tough consequence. That is why they also need to be punished more severely, even if it has to be followed up by other things. For the punishments, there must be more. The Left will enter social control directly into the criminal code in the same way as hate crimes, so that it becomes an aggravating circumstance in the punishment when social control has been exercised as part of a criminal relationship. The Courts must be able to attach greater importance to social control, irrespective of whether social control is directed at women or men or whether there is psychological or physical violence. The legislation must make it clear that Denmark does not accept this, and that is what we do best by writing social control directly into the criminal code. In October I had the Minister in consultation on this subject and the major problems created by social control. I also presented this proposal to the Minister, but unfortunately at that time I could not get a clear and clear answer that supported the Left's proposal. And after today I have to say that I feel more certain that, although the proposals are similar to each other, the government's bill and the motion for a resolution tabled by the Left are therefore more far-reaching; ours is to go further. Finally, I would just like to say thank you to the parties that have supported it. Thanks to DF and Enhedslisten, Conservative and New Citizens. To be honest, I was not so surprised that my blue friends and comrades, Pernille Vermund, Marie Krarup and Marcus Knuth, backed up. Unfortunately, Henrik Dahl had to leave, but of course they also support our proposal. Now I see that the rapporteur to whom I would like to speak has gone, unfortunately. Maybe they're watching. However, I would like to make an appeal for them to look again at the motion for a resolution tabled by the Left and the government's legislative proposals. Otherwise, I would also just like to say that my door is open for further talk. Thank you for the floor.

ParlaMint-DK_20201217190920	I would also like to start by expressing my thanks for the motion for a resolution B 92 on safeguarding and preserving the Danish cultural tradition in public spaces, and I would like to start by saying that we in the government cannot support the resolution. Because we are experiencing examples of forces that do not recognise democracy and absolutely fundamental rights and rules of play in this society. It is a set of values which should not take root, or which, I am sorry to say, should not be allowed to take root deeper in Danish society than it has already done, because it is devastating for integration and for cohesion. So, as I said, I actually understand the concerns here and think it is also a relevant discussion. I also think it is important that we constantly ask ourselves what values our society should base itself on and reflect. I myself am rounded by values of community, cohesion and everything that makes society function from my position. For me, therefore, it is basically about how newcomers become citizens and not against citizens. The motion for a resolution refers to the national interests of the Planning Act as a reason for a state objection to proposals for municipal planning. For example: It is a national interest that the municipalities take into account in their planning cultural historical conservation values which relate to the traces of human activity in the open country and cities from prehistoric to recent times, for example. the churches are distinctive cultural historical distinguishing marks in the landscape. However, the fact that there is room in municipal planning for activities that are not considered to arise from Danish cultural values cannot in itself justify an objection under the Planning Act. Questions have previously been raised about the possibility of ensuring with local planning that mosques and other ecclesiastical activities are placed in places suitable for it. The Minister for Industry has informed me that he has asked his ministry to investigate whether and, if so, how the Planning Act can contribute in such a context, so that it can be included in future political discussions as a follow-up to the Plan Act evaluation. I do not think that we should be opposed in principle to mosques. In my view, it is not the case that you put a few bricks on top of each other and call it a mosque that is the problem. We have plenty of religious, including Muslim, citizens who are well-functioning and constructive fellow citizens of Danish society. One of the fundamental rights in Denmark is freedom of religion, which means that one can freely cultivate one's faith in Denmark, just as one can gather to cultivate one's faith in a way that is consistent with one's beliefs. This means that there is nothing to prevent a Muslim, for example. arranges a bathroom or a mosque in an existing building, or that a Buddhist arranges a temple in an existing building, provided, of course, that applicable legislation and regulation in the area are complied with. The problem arises when extremist ideas propagate into ethnic minority communities and when, for example, A mosque becomes a distribution center for destructive ideas. That is why we also have to ask ourselves questions such as:: Who has financed the mosque, and what is being preached about? After all, the government came up with a bill in November on the ban on receiving donations from legal persons, including foreign state authorities, government-controlled organisations and companies that oppose or undermine democracy and fundamental freedoms and human rights. The government also plans to table a bill on sermons in languages other than Danish. The purpose of this bill is to create greater openness about religious preachers' sermons in Denmark when they preach in languages other than Danish. It's the church minister who's bringing that bill. I would also like to mention briefly the debate on prayer calls, which we have had here in the Chamber, when I also said at that time that I think prayer calls are presumptuous for the citizens who live nearby. The government has therefore taken the initiative to clarify the legal basis for a ban on prayer calls, so we know the response options. That work is still under way. It is important for me to stress that the proposed rules will of course apply to all citizens of Denmark regardless of religious affiliation. The proposal that has been put forward today also provides an opportunity to remember some of the tools that we already have the opportunity to use to combat those who will challenge our democracy and our values. It may be the national list of sanctions, which is a tool that helps prevent hate preachers from entering Denmark. I can also mention the parallel community agreement, which was adopted in spring 2018, and which the Social Democracy also helped to agree with the then government. That agreement is also an important step in the fight to bring Denmark together. The agreement must ensure that there are no longer ghettos in Denmark by 2030. The latest list from the Minister of Housing shows that it is going in the right direction... the effort is actually working. The government is also prepared to see whether other measures can be taken to prevent religious parallel societies, and we welcome all good proposals. In conclusion, I would like to mention that we are also in a good dialogue with European colleagues who are facing the same challenges as we are doing and where we share experiences, among other things. on how to prevent anti-democratic funding of activity in European countries. Thank you for the floor.

When it comes to the architectural expression itself, we believe that it must be up to the municipalities themselves to decide on this. ParlaMint-DK_20201217203126	Thank you for the floor. In the Danish People's Party we are tired of cases of building storm mosques and of noisy Islamic prayer calls. We are tired of them because there is so much uncertainty about what you can do to avoid such things in local communities, and because each city council has to invent the deep plate to find out how to handle something that everyone can see doesn't belong in Denmark at all. Although we have a Muslim minority in Denmark, we should not have the country transformed into a copy of the Middle East. Denmark is a Christian country. There's a cross in the brook. Christianity has made its mark in the way we have shaped our society, and it puts its obvious physical and cultural traces in public space. Churches, for example, are... built so that they lie at the highest points of the landscape and are often subject to protection, so that new buildings and masters should not shadow this cultural heritage. It is right and proper that Danish culture and values leave their clear mark on our everyday lives, and they must continue to do so. During our attempts to get noisy Islamic prayer calls banned in Denmark, we received an interesting reply from the Minister of Business that prayer calls could not be banned because prayer calls were not included in section 15 of the Plan Act. 2, there is the paragraph that regulates what local plans may include. So because of this, each city council stands rather defenseless when it comes to making a local plan to contain prayer calls or some other form of Islamization of public space. Therefore, we had the idea that it should be included in the planning law that Denmark should, of course, be able to maintain its cultural traditions. Planning legislation deals with four national interests to be taken into account in particular. These are (1) growth and industrial development; (2) natural and environmental protection; (3) cultural heritage and landscape conservation; and (4) national and regional facilities. In the Danish People's Party we believe that maintaining the Danish cultural tradition should be a fundamental national consideration and thus be included in the planning legislation as the first of the special considerations that can be taken into account when drawing up a local plan for an area. The existing provisions aim to preserve special cultural heritage for posterity, but they do not take into account the fact that we must preserve Danish society and cultural activities for posterity. They are primarily aimed at buildings. The regulations are largely related to the regulation of the physical expression and, to a lesser extent, to the content or purpose of the activities to be carried out within the framework of the Planning Act. There is therefore a need for the law of plans to be amended, for example. in Section 15, paragraphs (a), (b), (c), (d) and (e). 2, so local plans relate to ensuring the Danish cultural tradition, because it cannot be right that in Denmark, which is so much based on Christian democratic values, we cannot regulate and limit what our country should look like. In our opinion, it is crucial that Danish culture and Danish values become an overall consideration and that it can be regulated both nationally and locally, so for example. loudspeaker reinforced prayer calls do not take place without prior permission. We do not believe that this motion for a resolution is in any way a problem for freedom of religion, nor do we believe that this resolution should ban mosques in general. I don't think you can or should. But we believe that it provides a tool for banning Islamic prayer calls from among others. minarets and construction of large mosques in local areas. We must ensure that the Danish culture and values in the same way as the church occupy the highest point and maintain its special position in Danish society. Thank you for today's debate. I am, of course, really sorry that so many people will not support it. We could really expect it to be supported. This is true, for example. The Conservative People's Party, but the Conservative People's Party and the Left have found some commas and perhaps also some spelling mistakes and some practical details that make it just impossible to support it. Of course, we think it is a pity if it is the case that they actually think that Danish culture should be maintained in Denmark. But we are happy to come back with a more precise proposal for amending the law on plans. Then there are others who have said that the proposal calls into question freedom of religion. I have already said that it does not. This is not a reasonable objection to it, as well as the flag law does not affect freedom of religion, but one can only flag with a formbrog in Denmark unless one gets a special permit. The same is true of the law we have regarding church buildings, that you must not build higher than the church right next door or shadow for the church. Nor does it attack freedom of religion in Denmark. We already have these kinds of rules, and that is actually the thinking we want to spread to the law of plans, so that it will be easier for each city council to take a position on the Islamisation that is taking place around the local communities. But we'd be happy to take it up again. We are happy to take it up in the Business Committee so that all those who have found hair in the soup will find it harder to find hair in the soup. ParlaMint-DK_20201217204524	The debate is open. The first rapporteur comes from the Social Democratic Party, and that is Mr. Rasmus Stoklund.
ParlaMint-DK_20201217204541	Thank you. I will start by saying that I am here today as a substitute for our transport rapporteur, Thomas Jensen, who is prevented. In the context of the modernisation of the Taxi Act, which entered into force on 1 January 2018, the residence requirement was continued as a requirement for an undertaking to have an ownership group resident in the EU or the EEA in order for it to be granted an authorisation for commercial passenger transport or an authorisation to operate a driving office. The purpose of the residence requirement was to make it possible to monitor whether an undertaking meets the requirements of good practice in the industry. In a supervisory case in December 2019, it was found that the residence requirement had, on the one hand, too far-reaching consequences when the company was operating in company form and, on the other hand, was not appropriate to achieve the objective of facilitating the supervision of the company's good repute. Among other things: Against this background, the specific undertaking which was the subject of the surveillance proceedings raised questions as to whether the residence requirement was in conformity with the Treaty on the Functioning of the European Union on freedom of establishment and freedom of movement for capital. Following a closer examination of this question, among other things. The Attorney General was asked for advice, the Minister of Transport decided, in agreement with the agreement circle behind the Taxi Act, the Left, the Danish People's Party, the Conservative People's Party, the Liberal Alliance, the Radical Left, the Social Democratic Party and the SF in December 2019 to suspend enforcement of the residence requirement in accordance with the principle of the primacy of EU law. Therefore, since December 2019 the residence requirement has not been enforced in connection with the processing of applications for authorisation for commercial passenger transport and authorisation to operate a driving office. This bill therefore aims to bring the law into line with the legal situation that has been in practice since December 2019 as regards the conditions for the residence of the owner of the company in the EU for the authorisation of commercial passenger transport or the authorisation to operate a driving office, as the residence requirement is now removed from the law. Subsequently, the Ministry of Transport and Housing has examined whether the residence requirement in the EU/EEA in respect of drivers and responsible managers of taxi companies and driving offices respectively can be said to have too far-reaching consequences in relation to the purpose and that, accordingly, the compatibility of these requirements with Union law can be questioned accordingly. This has proved to be the case and, for that reason, it is proposed that these requirements should also be deleted from the law. In addition, the bill contains a large number of technical changes to the taxi law, in view of the repeal of the EU/EEA residence requirement provisions. Social democracy can support the proposal, and I have also promised to welcome the radical rapporteur and to say that the Radical Left also supports the proposal.

ParlaMint-DK_20201217211134	Thank you for that, President, and thank you very much for the opportunity to discuss this important issue. New citizens have tabled a motion for a resolution which will oblige the government to put forward legislative proposals to organise an indicative referendum on Denmark's accession to the EU recovery plan. As part of the negotiations on the EU's multiannual financial framework, what is known as MFF is also being introduced in popular terms, a new recovery plan is being introduced to support the recovery of European economies. The total amount of the recovery plan is 750 billion. EUR distributed in the form of either direct grants or loans. Direct grants amount to 390 billion. EUR, and the remaining EUR 360 billion. The euro will be used as a loan to the Member States. A smaller proportion, approx. 80 billion. Euro, is channelled into a number of EU spending programmes such as research and rural support via the EU budget, while the majority, i.e. approx. 670 billion The euro will be implemented through the so-called recovery facility, which will be distributed either as direct grants or on-lending. It is no secret that the government in the negotiations was and for that matter still is extremely sceptical about direct subsidies. That is why we worked hard in the negotiations to ensure that the funds could be made available as loans on favourable terms. We did so because we believe that it is a healthy principle to repay your own debt. That is why we have always been sceptical about transfers between countries financed by common debt. At the same time, however, this is a situation in which, among 27 countries, we had to obtain unanimity. In other words, it was very difficult negotiations, how many interests were at stake, of course. For the government it was essential to ensure that the recovery plan became as responsible as possible. Given the very different positions of the countries, we therefore worked hard to reduce the proportion of direct grants. But, of course, it was also for the government that the recovery instrument should contribute to digitisation and the green transition. The green elements of the recovery plan must therefore be implemented so that they support the European Green Pact • also what is called green deal in EU contexts. And then, of course, it was a question of ensuring that this is a one-off case, a crisis aid which does not fall into the general budget and which has a clear expiry date appropriate to the temporary crisis. The government's efforts in the negotiations must be said to have borne fruit. We had reduced the share of direct grants from 500 billion. EUR 390 billion proposed by the Commission. EUR 110 billion. euro. In addition, the funds from the EU recovery plan, together with the MFF, are included in the requirement that at least 30% of the funds be spent. must go to climate initiatives. This is important in order to support a green transition, which must therefore also help restore growth. It is also important for the government that recovery funds are used sensibly. That is why, of course, we are also pleased that all countries need to draw up a recovery plan in order to have access to the funds in the EU recovery facility. The plans of the countries must be approved by the Council on the basis of an assessment by the European Commission. A number of criteria must be met in order to make funds available to the Member States. Firstly, the recovery plans must address the country-specific recommendations of the European Semester; secondly, the recovery plans must address the covid-19 pandemic through strengthened growth potential, job creation and economic and social resilience; thirdly, at least 37 per cent. the funds are earmarked for the green conversion; and finally, at least 20% of the funds are earmarked for the green conversion; and fourthly, at least 20% of the funds are earmarked for the green conversion. the funds are earmarked for digitisation. These measures ensure that the recovery plan contributes to economic recovery in Europe in both the short and the long term. I also understand that the proposers are concerned that the EU recovery plan will increase the pressure and need for EU taxes. On this issue, the government's position is quite clear: we do not want to give the EU the powers to issue taxes. It belongs in the Member States. That is why, in the negotiating table for the MFF, we also attached the utmost importance to the fact that new sources of income are collected in accordance with the provisions of the Treaty and are therefore not based on direct collections from citizens or companies from the European Union. In addition, let me make it quite clear that the sources of income included in the MFF agreement are not EU taxes. As far as the new space contribution is concerned, it is basically a redistribution of EU contributions that underpins the green transition. The compromise with the European Parliament sets out a timetable for the presentation by the Commission of proposals for new own resources. When there is a proposal for new sources of revenue, it still needs to be dealt with on an equal footing with other EU proposals. This implies, by its very nature, the involvement of the Parliamentary Committee on Europe. It remains the case that the introduction of new revenue into the EU budget requires unanimity among the Member States. The agreement therefore does not bind the Member States to vote in favour of concrete proposals. Let me say once again that it remains essential for the government to retain the exclusive right of Member States to collect taxes on the part of citizens and businesses. In other words, the government has delivered an agreement which is fully in line with the mandate which a majority of Parliament's parties supported at the time. It is an agreement which is solidarity and which supports the necessary steps to help restore the European economies. It is also an agreement that is of enormous importance for the internal market and therefore also for Denmark's main export market. The EU recovery plan is at the heart of Europe's recovery from the corona crisis. Up to 600,000 Danish jobs depend on exports to the EU, and the economic situation throughout the EU is therefore of enormous importance for Denmark, Danish companies and Danish employees. It is therefore also in Denmark's clear interest that the economic consequences of the corona crisis will be as few and as short as possible. At the same time, let me stress that we in Denmark do not have a tradition of issuing indicative referendums, and I do not see why this matter should break with this. Against this background, of course, the government cannot support the motion for a resolution.
ParlaMint-DK_20201217211924	Thank you, Minister. There are a few brief comments to be made to the Minister. First, it's sir. Peter Seier Christensen, New Citizens. Here you go.

ParlaMint-DK_20201217220248	Thank you. There are four reasons why Enhedslisten supports this proposal for a referendum. First of all, and this has been mentioned by several people, it imposes obligations on Denmark for generations to come. It is true that there are other things that do too, but after all it is not every day that we make decisions in here that bind the economy so much up to 2058. Secondly, there is no doubt that the fact that all countries in the European Union are subject to this payment increases the pressure for own resources to be established in the European Union at a very different level than we know today. Homemell will be found. There, of course, we have seen creative things time and again, and with the interpretation given in the Ministry of Justice in relation to the surrender of sovereignty, I have no doubt that it is possible to get quite a long way along the way without requiring a referendum in Denmark. But we want to see massive pressure, because countries that are economically disadvantaged have to draw the payments from their annual budgets, and it is clear that then the idea of own resources for the European Union will come. This is something that we are opposed to, and we can explain why we are. Thirdly, we are in favour of a referendum because we are against the recovery package. We are not against it in general. We actually presented our own recovery package. So the idea of borrowing money together to help the poorest countries seems to us to be very good, but there were three crucial problems in this recovery package. One was that to some extent it would benefit the wrong countries, which were not affected by corona, even though that was the starting point. The second is that the content is not green enough. And then you can say that it's typically Enhedslisten not green enough. Yes, but we have a 70-percent target, and there are limits to how many times we can go out and borrow so many hundred billion dollars. There are limits to how many times we can do this to get the green transition in place. Therefore, this opportunity should have been used to ensure the green transition. You don't. The third is, and this is for the SF's rapporteur, that there are no guarantees that this will not be used to impose neoliberal policies. I am not criticising the SF rapporteur for not reading El País, nor am I daily, but I have been given the quote. It tells us what these are, at least at this stage, rumours that the Commission has raised in the discussions with the Spanish Government. And one of the things the Commission has raised is that the prerequisite for paying out money from the Recovery Fund is that Spanish pensions do not follow price developments. It can also be said more directly: Spanish pensioners must, day by day, become poorer and poorer compared with the rest of the population. Yes, that is what is written in El País, i.e. that it is negotiated in government offices. Another point that is mentioned is the question of trade union rights, which was dropped by the bourgeois government and which the new government wanted to reinstate, but trade union rights must not be reinstated. It can then be said that this can really happen, because I am sure that good forces, such as the Social Democrats and the SF, have been given a guarantee that under no circumstances could this be used to restore this neoliberal policy of cuts to employees. But unfortunately, there is no such thing in the agreement. So there is one more reason why we cannot support the recovery package and therefore also believe that it should be blocked and therefore wants a referendum. The fourth and final argument is that we were never allowed to vote on the Lisbon Treaty. So, the basis for the whole regime on which the European Union is based today is the Treaty of Lisbon, and the Danish people have never been allowed to vote on it. So I have a fresh exchange for the minister: okay, we drop the vote on the recovery package, and then we vote on the Lisbon Treaty, so that the basis on which the EU is working has popular support, just as it did when it voted on the Maastricht Treaty and later on the Edinburgh Agreement. We think it is absolutely essential that there is that support, and we cannot see it today. That is why it is clear that, when there is an opportunity to give the public an opportunity to take a position with all three other arguments I have mentioned, then, of course, we support it. That is why we support the proposal.
